<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1066479718"><table class="infobox vcard"><tbody><tr><th colspan="2" class="infobox-above" style="font-weight: normal; background-color: gold;"><span style="font-size:80%">Papal styles of</span><br /><b><span class="fn">Pope Clement VI</span></b></th></tr><tr><td colspan="2" class="infobox-image"><span typeof="mw:File"><a href="/wiki/File:C_o_a_Beaufort_Popes.svg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/27/C_o_a_Beaufort_Popes.svg/60px-C_o_a_Beaufort_Popes.svg.png" decoding="async" width="60" height="72" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/27/C_o_a_Beaufort_Popes.svg/90px-C_o_a_Beaufort_Popes.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/27/C_o_a_Beaufort_Popes.svg/120px-C_o_a_Beaufort_Popes.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="1031" data-file-height="1238" /></a></span></td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label"><a href="/wiki/Style_(manner_of_address)" class="mw-redirect" title="Style (manner of address)">Reference style</a></th><td class="infobox-data"><a href="/wiki/His_Holiness" class="mw-redirect" title="His Holiness">His Holiness</a></td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Spoken style</th><td class="infobox-data">Your Holiness</td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Religious style</th><td class="infobox-data">Holy Father</td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Posthumous style</th><td class="infobox-data">None</td></tr></tbody></table>
<p><b>Pope Clement VI</b> (<a href="/wiki/Latin_language" class="mw-redirect" title="Latin language">Latin</a>: <i lang="la">Clemens VI</i>; 1291 – 6 December 1352), born <b>Pierre Roger</b>,<sup id="cite_ref-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup> was head of the <a href="/wiki/Catholic_Church" title="Catholic Church">Catholic Church</a> from 7 May 1342 to his death, in December 1352. He was the fourth <a href="/wiki/Avignon_Papacy" title="Avignon Papacy">Avignon pope</a>. Clement reigned during the first visitation of the <a href="/wiki/Black_Death" title="Black Death">Black Death</a> (1348–1350), during which he granted remission of sins to all who died of the plague.
</p><p>Roger steadfastly resisted temporal encroachments on the Church's <a href="/wiki/Ecclesiastical_jurisdiction" title="Ecclesiastical jurisdiction">ecclesiastical jurisdiction</a> and, as Clement VI, entrenched French dominance of the Church and opened its coffers to enhance the regal splendour of the Papacy. He recruited composers and music theorists for his court, including figures associated with the then-innovative <a href="/wiki/Ars_Nova" class="mw-redirect" title="Ars Nova">Ars Nova</a> style of France and the <a href="/wiki/Low_Countries" title="Low Countries">Low Countries</a>.
<span class="anchor" id="Biography"></span><span class="anchor" id="History"></span>
</p>
<meta property="mw:PageProp/toc" />
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Early_life">Early life</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Clement_VI&amp;action=edit&amp;section=1" title="Edit section: Early life">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Birth_and_family">Birth and family</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Clement_VI&amp;action=edit&amp;section=2" title="Edit section: Birth and family">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Pierre Roger (also spelled Rogier and Rosiers) was born in the <a href="/wiki/Ch%C3%A2teau" title="Château">château</a> of <a href="/wiki/Communes_of_the_Corr%C3%A8ze_department" title="Communes of the Corrèze department">Maumont</a>, today part of the commune of <a href="/wiki/Rosiers-d%27%C3%89gletons" title="Rosiers-d&#39;Égletons">Rosiers-d'Égletons</a>, <a href="/wiki/Corr%C3%A8ze" title="Corrèze">Corrèze</a>, in <a href="/wiki/Limousin_(province)" title="Limousin (province)">Limousin</a>, France, the son of the lord of Maumont-Rosiers-d'Égletons.  He had an elder brother, Guillaume, who married three times and had thirteen children; and a younger brother, Hugues, who became Cardinal Priest of S. Lorenzo in Damaso and who could have become pope in 1362.  Pierre also had two sisters: Delphine, who married Jacques de Besse; and Alienor, who married Jacques de la Jugie.  His brother Guillaume became Seigneur de Chambon, thanks to his wife's dowry, and, with the benefit of his papal brother's influence on King Philip VI, became Vicomte de Beaufort.<sup id="cite_ref-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Monk_and_scholar">Monk and scholar</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Clement_VI&amp;action=edit&amp;section=3" title="Edit section: Monk and scholar">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Roger entered the <a href="/wiki/Order_of_Saint_Benedict" class="mw-redirect" title="Order of Saint Benedict">Benedictine order</a><sup id="cite_ref-McBrien240_3-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-McBrien240-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup> as a boy in 1301,  at the Abbey of <a href="/wiki/La_Chaise-Dieu" title="La Chaise-Dieu">La Chaise-Dieu</a> in the diocese of Clermont in the Auvergne.<sup id="cite_ref-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-4">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup>  After six years there, he was directed to higher studies by the Bishop of Le Puy, Jean de Cumenis, and his own abbot, Hugues d'Arc.<sup id="cite_ref-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-5">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup>  In 1307 he took up studies in Paris at the <a href="/wiki/College_de_Sorbonne" class="mw-redirect" title="College de Sorbonne">College de Sorbonne</a>, where he entered the Collège de Narbonne.  To support him, beyond what was supplied by his bishop and his abbot, he was granted the post of Prior of St. Pantaléon in the diocese of Limoges.<sup id="cite_ref-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup>  In the summer of 1323, after Pierre had been studying both theology and canon law<sup id="cite_ref-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup> in Paris for sixteen years, the Chancellor of Paris was ordered by Pope John XXII, on the recommendation of King Charles IV, to confer on him the doctorate in Theology, a chair, and a license to teach.<sup id="cite_ref-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-8">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup>  Pierre was in his thirty-first year.<sup id="cite_ref-9" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-9">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup> He lectured publicly on the <i>Sententiae</i> of <a href="/wiki/Peter_Lombard" title="Peter Lombard">Peter Lombard</a>, and defended and promoted the works of <a href="/wiki/Thomas_Aquinas" title="Thomas Aquinas">Thomas Aquinas</a>.  He was appalled by the <i><a href="/wiki/Defensor_Pacis" class="mw-redirect" title="Defensor Pacis">Defensor Pacis</a></i> of Marsilius of Padua, and wrote a treatise in 1325 condemning its principles and defending Pope John XXII.<sup id="cite_ref-10" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>He was granted the priory of St. Baudil, a dependency of the Abbey of La Chaise-Dieu, on 24 April 1324, at the personal order of <a href="/wiki/Pope_John_XXII" title="Pope John XXII">Pope John XXII</a>; and then, on 23 June 1326, he was named <a href="/wiki/Abbot_of_F%C3%A9camp" class="mw-redirect" title="Abbot of Fécamp">Abbot of Fécamp</a>, a royal abbey and one of the most important monasteries in France.  He held the position until 1329.<sup id="cite_ref-11" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-11">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Pierre Roger was called to Avignon through the influence of his friend and protector, Cardinal Pierre de Mortemart (who was named a cardinal on 18 December 1327), both of whom were close to King Charles IV.<sup id="cite_ref-12" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-12">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup>  Unfortunately, King Charles IV died on 1 February 1328, the last Capetian king of France in the direct line.
</p><p>As Abbot of Fécamp, and therefore a feudal subject of Edward III, Pierre  was assigned the task in 1328 of summoning <a href="/wiki/Edward_III_of_England" title="Edward III of England">Edward III of England</a> to pay homage to <a href="/wiki/Philip_VI_of_France" title="Philip VI of France">Philip VI of France</a> for the <a href="/wiki/Duchy_of_Aquitaine" title="Duchy of Aquitaine">duchy of Aquitaine</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-13" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-13">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup>  He received no reply, however, from King Edward, and was forced to return to France, his mission unaccomplished.<sup id="cite_ref-14" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-14">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Episcopacy">Episcopacy</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Clement_VI&amp;action=edit&amp;section=4" title="Edit section: Episcopacy">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>On 3 December 1328<sup id="cite_ref-15" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-15">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup> Peter Roger was named <a href="/wiki/Bishop_of_Arras" class="mw-redirect" title="Bishop of Arras">Bishop of Arras</a>, in which capacity he became a royal councilor of King Philip VI.<sup id="cite_ref-16" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-16">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup> He held the diocese of Arras only until 24 November 1329, less than a year, when he was promoted to the <a href="/wiki/Archbishop_of_Sens" class="mw-redirect" title="Archbishop of Sens">Archdiocese of Sens</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-17" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-17">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup>  He held the Archbishopric of Sens for one year and one month, until his promotion to the See of <a href="/wiki/Archbishop_of_Rouen" class="mw-redirect" title="Archbishop of Rouen">Rouen</a> on 14 December 1330.<sup id="cite_ref-18" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-18">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1329, while Pierre Roger was still Archbishop-elect of Sens, a major assembly of the French Clergy was held at Vincennes in the presence of <a href="/wiki/Philip_VI_of_France" title="Philip VI of France">King Philip VI</a> (1328–1350), to deal with issues involving the judicial powers of ecclesiastical authorities.  Many propositions were put forward against ecclesiastical jurisdiction, which were ably argued by Pierre de Cugnières (Petrus de Cugneriis).  Pierre Roger made the rejoinders on 22 December 1329, on behalf of the ecclesiastical authority.<sup id="cite_ref-19" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-19">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>When Pierre Roger became Archbishop of Rouen in December 1330, he was expected to swear allegiance to his feudal overlord.  King Philip VI had recently given his son Jean the Dukedom of Normandy as an apanage, and Pierre was worried about what might happen if someone other than a member of the French royal family might become Duke of Normandy.  He therefore asked the King for time to consider his position, but the King was firm and seized the temporalities of the Archbishop.  Pierre was forced to go to Paris, where an agreement was worked out that, should someone other than a member of the royal family become Duke, then the Archbishop would swear fealty directly to the King.<sup id="cite_ref-20" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>As Archbishop of Rouen, Roger was one of the Peers of France and he was a member of the embassy sent by King Philip to his son <a href="/wiki/John_II_of_France" title="John II of France">John</a>, in 1333, to swear in their name to take the cross and serve in a crusade in the Holy Land.  Later in the year, in Paris in the Prés des Clercs, the King received the cross personally from the hands of Archbishop Roger.<sup id="cite_ref-21" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-21">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>It is said that he was promoted to the office of <a href="/wiki/Chancellor_of_France" title="Chancellor of France">Chancellor of France</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-McBrien240_3-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-McBrien240-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup> though there is no documentary proof.<sup id="cite_ref-22" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-22">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup>  The earliest claim that he was Chancellor is made by <a href="/wiki/Alphonsus_Ciacconius" title="Alphonsus Ciacconius">Alfonso Chacon</a> (Ciaconius) (1530–1599).<sup id="cite_ref-23" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-23">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Gallia_christiana_XI,_p._77_24-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Gallia_christiana_XI,_p._77-24">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1333, the issue of the <a href="/wiki/Beatific_vision" title="Beatific vision">beatific vision</a>, which had been under discussion since a sermon of Pope John XXII in 1329, reached a serious stage.<sup id="cite_ref-25" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-25">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup>  The French Royal Court had been hearing complaints from various quarters, and the King and Queen finally decided to seek competent advice.  The Pope knew that the University of Paris was hostile to his ideas, and so he sent Gerard Odonis, the Minister General of the Franciscans,<sup id="cite_ref-26" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-26">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup> and a Dominican preacher, to Paris to preach the Pope's views in public.  King Philip responded to the general indignation by summoning the Masters of Theology of the University to Vincennes just before Christmas 1334, where it appeared that there was general agreement against the Pope.  The King privately informed the Pope of their opinions, but the Pope harshly responded to the King that he should stop favoring an opinion which the Pope had not yet definitively settled.  The Pope ordered the Archbishop of Rouen, Pierre Roger, to set the Pope's view down in writing and explain it to the King.  Ironically, Pierre Roger was not on the Pope's side of the argument. A committee, which included Archbishop Roger, the theologian Pierre de la Palud (Petrus Paludensis), the Chancellor of France Guillaume de Sainte-Maure, the Archdeacon of Rouen Jean de Polenciac, and others, attempted to talk the Pope out of his notions.<sup id="cite_ref-Gallia_christiana_XI,_p._77_24-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Gallia_christiana_XI,_p._77-24">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup> Early in 1334 Pope John XXII informed the King that he had ordered the Cardinals and prelates and Doctors of theology and of Canon Law at the Papal Court to look into the propositions thoroughly and report to him their findings.<sup id="cite_ref-27" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-27">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup>  John XXII was attempting to save face by placing the matter in the hands of a committee, but in the end, on his deathbed, he was compelled to repudiate his opinions, which were formally condemned by his successor, Benedict XII.<sup id="cite_ref-28" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-28">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On 14 April 1335, Pierre Roger's friend and patron, Cardinal Pierre de Mortemart died, naming Pierre Roger as one of the executors of his Testament.<sup id="cite_ref-29" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-29">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In September 1335 Archbishop Roger held a provincial council at Rouen in the Priory of Nôtre-Dame-du Pré (later called Bonne-nouvelle).<sup id="cite_ref-30" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-30">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup> Two of his bishops were present, the other four were represented by procurators. The cathedral chapters of the province and the abbots of monasteries were invited as well.<sup id="cite_ref-31" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-31">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup>  The council issued a dozen canons, urging the lower clergy to be diligent in their assigned duties.  The most notable item was the encouragement given to bishops to facilitate the business of those who wished to join the King on crusade.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Cardinalate">Cardinalate</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Clement_VI&amp;action=edit&amp;section=5" title="Edit section: Cardinalate">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Pierre Roger was created a <a href="/wiki/Cardinal-priest" class="mw-redirect" title="Cardinal-priest">Cardinal Priest</a> by <a href="/wiki/Pope_Benedict_XII" title="Pope Benedict XII">Pope Benedict XII</a> (1334–1342) on 18 December 1338, in his only Consistory for the creation of cardinals.  He created six new cardinals: four were fellow monks (two Benedictines, a Cistercian, and a Mercedarian); one was from Rimini, the rest from southern France. Four were lawyers, two were theologians. One died before he received the red hat, and was replaced by another candidate.  Pierre Roger entered the Curia in Avignon for the first time on 5 May 1339, and received the <a href="/wiki/Titular_church" title="Titular church"><i>titulus</i></a> of <a href="/wiki/Santi_Nereo_e_Achilleo" title="Santi Nereo e Achilleo">Santi Nereo e Achilleo</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-32" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-32">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Papacy">Papacy</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Clement_VI&amp;action=edit&amp;section=6" title="Edit section: Papacy">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>Cardinal Napoleone Orsini died during Lent of 1342, on 23 March.  The funeral took place on Monday in Holy Week in the Franciscan church in Avignon, and the funeral sermon was preached by Cardinal Pierre Roger.<sup id="cite_ref-33" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-33">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup>  A month later, on 25 April 1342, Pope Benedict XII died in the Papal Palace in Avignon. King Philip VI immediately sent his eldest son, Prince John, to press the candidacy of Pierre Roger, but he arrived too late to have any effect.<sup id="cite_ref-34" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-34">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup> Eighteen of the nineteen cardinals assembled for the <a href="/wiki/Papal_conclave,_1342" class="mw-redirect" title="Papal conclave, 1342">Conclave</a> to elect his successor. Fourteen were French, three were Italian, one was Spanish. Only Cardinal Bertrand de Montfavez, who was ill with podagra (gout), was unable to attend.  The Conclave began on Sunday, 5 May 1342, and on the morning of Tuesday, 7 May, agreement was reached. Two cardinals wrote to King Edward III of England on 8 May that the election had been accomplished "with no preliminary politicking and with only Divine Inspiration."<sup id="cite_ref-35" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-35">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup>  Cardinal Pierre Roger was chosen to succeed Benedict XII as pope.<sup id="cite_ref-36" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-36">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup>   He was crowned on Pentecost Sunday, 19 May, in the church of the Dominicans, the largest church in Avignon.  Present were Prince John of France, Duke of Normandy; Jacques, Duke of Burgundy, Imbert,  Dauphin of Vienne, and many others.  Cardinal Roger chose the regnal name Clement VI.
</p><p>During the season of Pentecost immediately following his coronation, as Peter de Herenthal writes,<sup id="cite_ref-37" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-37">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup> when a new Pope customarily gratifies the expectations of his family, his followers, his supporters, his cardinals, and the Roman Curia,  Pope Clement promised gifts to every cleric who presented himself at Avignon within two months.<sup id="cite_ref-38" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-38">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup> Such a multitude of poor clerics appeared in Avignon that a computation was made that the number of poor clerics in all the dioceses of the world was around 100,000,  a number which Peter de Herenthal was quite prepared to accept. When Clement VI, at the very beginning of his pontificate was making reservations of abbacies and prelatures,  and declaring elections in monasteries and Chapters void, in order to acquire benefices for papal use in granting favors, it was intimated to him that his predecessors had not engaged in reservations of such a sort. Clement is said to have replied, "Our predecessors did not know how to be pope."<sup id="cite_ref-39" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-39">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="New_cardinals">New cardinals</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Clement_VI&amp;action=edit&amp;section=7" title="Edit section: New cardinals">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>One of the greatest ways in which a pope can reward his supporters is to raise them to the cardinalate.  On 20 September 1342, four months after his coronation, Clement VI held a Consistory for the creation of cardinals.  He appointed ten prelates, including three nephews, Hugues Roger, Ademar Roberti<sup id="cite_ref-40" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-40">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup> and Bernard de la Tour d'Auvergne.<sup id="cite_ref-41" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-41">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup>  He also elevated <a href="/wiki/Guy_of_Boulogne" title="Guy of Boulogne">Guy of Boulogne</a>, the archbishop of Lyon and son of Count <a href="/w/index.php?title=Robert_VII_of_Auvergne&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Robert VII of Auvergne (page does not exist)">Robert VII of Auvergne</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Gerard_de_Daumar" title="Gerard de Daumar">Gerard de Daumar</a>, the master-general of the Dominicans and a papal cousin,<sup id="cite_ref-42" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-42">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup> who died a year after his creation, on 27 September 1343.  Five of his appointments were from his own native area of Limoges and one from Périgueux.  Only one was Italian, Andrea Ghini Malpighi, a Florentine, who died on 2 June 1343.  The College of Cardinals was now thoroughly French, with a strong accent of the Auvergne.<sup id="cite_ref-Eubel,_I,_p._18_43-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Eubel,_I,_p._18-43">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On 19 May 1344 the two new cardinals who had died were replaced by two more Frenchmen: the Provençal <a href="/wiki/Pierre_Bertrand_de_Colombier" title="Pierre Bertrand de Colombier">Pierre Bertrand</a>, the nephew of Cardinal Pierre Bertrand; and <a href="/wiki/Nicolas_de_Besse" title="Nicolas de Besse">Nicolas de Besse</a>, yet another papal nephew.<sup id="cite_ref-Eubel,_I,_p._18_43-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Eubel,_I,_p._18-43">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Like his immediate predecessors, Clement was devoted to France, and he demonstrated his French sympathies by refusing a solemn invitation to return to <a href="/wiki/Rome" title="Rome">Rome</a> from the city's people, as well as from the poet <a href="/wiki/Petrarch" title="Petrarch">Petrarch</a>. To placate the Romans, however, Clement VI issued the bull <i>Unigenitus Dei filius</i> on 27 January 1343,<sup id="cite_ref-44" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-44">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-45" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-45">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup> reducing the interval between one Great Jubilee and the next from 100 years to 50 years.  In the document he elaborated for the first time the power of the pope in the use of <a href="/wiki/Indulgences" class="mw-redirect" title="Indulgences">indulgences</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Wood32-33_46-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Wood32-33-46">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup> This document would later be used by Cardinal Cajetan in the examination of <a href="/wiki/Martin_Luther" title="Martin Luther">Martin Luther</a> and his <a href="/wiki/95_Theses" class="mw-redirect" title="95 Theses">95 Theses</a> in his trial at Augsburg in 1518.<sup id="cite_ref-Wood32-33_46-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Wood32-33-46">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup>  By then, <i>Unigenitus</i> was firmly fixed in Canon Law, having been added in the collection called <i><a href="/wiki/Extravagantes" title="Extravagantes">Extravagantes</a></i>.<sup id="cite_ref-47" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-47">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On 23 February 1343 Pope Clement appointed Pons Saturninus as his "Provisor of Works of the Palace", thereby beginning a program of construction and decoration that continued throughout his reign.  It was immediately clear that the Pope had no intention of returning to Rome, and that he intended to provide offices and quarters for the various organs of the Roman Curia in the Palace.  Pope Benedict XII, his predecessor, had built a palace, sufficiently accommodating for a Cistercian monk, but Pierre Roger had spent much of his career at the French Court and had imbibed its tastes for far greater display and ceremony. The Pope was, after all, a sovereign, and Clement intended to live and work in an appropriate state.  He commissioned the new Tower of the Garde-Robe, the Audience (for the Auditors of the Rota), the new Papal Chapel and the grand staircase that led to it, and the Tour de la Gache (where the <i>Audientia contradictarum</i>, the appellate court for countersuits, had its offices).  He was also responsible for the two new entrance façades.<sup id="cite_ref-48" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-48">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>He also purchased the sovereignty of <a href="/wiki/Avignon" title="Avignon">Avignon</a> from Queen <a href="/wiki/Joan_I_of_Naples" class="mw-redirect" title="Joan I of Naples">Joan I of Naples</a> in 1348 for the sum of 80,000 crowns.<sup id="cite_ref-49" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-49">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="The_Black_Death">The Black Death</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Clement_VI&amp;action=edit&amp;section=8" title="Edit section: The Black Death">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Clement VI was on the papal throne when the <a href="/wiki/Black_Death" title="Black Death">Black Death</a> first struck Europe in 1347. This <a href="/wiki/Pandemic" title="Pandemic">pandemic</a> swept through Asia and the Middle East and into Europe between 1347 and 1350, and is believed to have killed between a third and two-thirds of <a href="/wiki/Medieval_demography" title="Medieval demography">Europe's population</a>. During the plague, Clement attributed the plague to divine wrath.<sup id="cite_ref-50" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-50">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup> But he also sought the opinions of astrologers for an explanation. <a href="/wiki/Johannes_de_Muris" title="Johannes de Muris">Johannes de Muris</a> was among the team "of three who drew up a treatise explaining the plague of 1348 by the conjunction of <a href="/wiki/Saturn" title="Saturn">Saturn</a>, <a href="/wiki/Jupiter" title="Jupiter">Jupiter</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Mars" title="Mars">Mars</a> in 1341"<sup id="cite_ref-51" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-51">&#91;51&#93;</a></sup> Clement VI's physicians advised him that surrounding himself with torches would block the plague. However, he soon became skeptical of this recommendation and stayed in Avignon supervising sick care, burials, and the pastoral care of the dying.<sup id="cite_ref-52" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-52">&#91;52&#93;</a></sup> He never contracted the disease, even though there was so much death around him that the cities ran out of ground for cemeteries, and he had to consecrate the entire Rhône River so that it could be considered holy ground and bodies could be thrown into it.<sup id="cite_ref-53" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-53">&#91;53&#93;</a></sup>  One of Pope Clement's physicians, <a href="/wiki/Gui_de_Chauliac" class="mw-redirect" title="Gui de Chauliac">Gui de Chauliac</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-54" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-54">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup> later wrote a book called the <i><a href="/wiki/Chirurgia_magna" title="Chirurgia magna">Chirurgia magna</a></i> (1363), in which he correctly distinguished between bubonic and pneumonic plague, based on his own observations of his patients and himself.
</p><p>Perhaps feeling the pressure of mortality, having lost no fewer than six cardinals in the year 1348 alone,<sup id="cite_ref-55" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-55">&#91;55&#93;</a></sup> Pope Clement VI named a new cardinal on 29 May 1348,  his nephew and namesake, <a href="/wiki/Pope_Gregory_XI" title="Pope Gregory XI">Pierre Roger de Beaufort</a>, who was not yet eighteen years old.<sup id="cite_ref-56" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-56">&#91;56&#93;</a></sup>  On 17 December 1350, he added twelve more cardinals, nine of them French and only three from Limoges, including two relatives, Guillaume d'Aigrefeuille and Pierre de Cros.<sup id="cite_ref-57" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-57">&#91;57&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Suspicion fell on the Jews for the plague, and <a href="/wiki/Pogrom" title="Pogrom">pogroms</a> erupted around Europe. Clement issued two <a href="/wiki/Papal_bulls" class="mw-redirect" title="Papal bulls">papal bulls</a> in 1348 (6 July and 26 September), the latter named <i>Quamvis Perfidiam</i>, which condemned the violence and said those who blamed the plague on the Jews had been "seduced by that liar, the Devil."<sup id="cite_ref-Skolnik1_58-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Skolnik1-58">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup> He went on to emphasise that "It cannot be true that the Jews, by such a heinous crime, are the cause or occasion of the plague, because through many parts of the world the same plague, by the hidden judgment of God, has afflicted and afflicts the Jews themselves and many other races who have never lived alongside them."<sup id="cite_ref-59" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-59">&#91;59&#93;</a></sup> He urged clergy to take action to protect Jews as he had done.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Pope_and_Empire">Pope and Empire</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Clement_VI&amp;action=edit&amp;section=9" title="Edit section: Pope and Empire">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Clement continued the struggle of his predecessors with <a href="/wiki/Holy_Roman_Emperor" title="Holy Roman Emperor">Holy Roman Emperor</a> <a href="/wiki/Louis_IV,_Holy_Roman_Emperor" title="Louis IV, Holy Roman Emperor">Louis IV</a>. On 13 April 1346, after protracted negotiations, he excommunicated the Emperor, and directed the election of <a href="/wiki/Charles_IV,_Holy_Roman_Emperor" title="Charles IV, Holy Roman Emperor">Charles IV</a>.  After the death of Louis in October 1347  Charles received general recognition, ending the schism which had long divided Germany.<sup id="cite_ref-EB1911_60-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-EB1911-60">&#91;60&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Clement proclaimed a crusade in 1343, but nothing was accomplished beyond a naval attack on <a href="/wiki/Smyrna" title="Smyrna">Smyrna</a> on 29 October 1344.<sup id="cite_ref-EB1911_60-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-EB1911-60">&#91;60&#93;</a></sup> He also had a role in the <a href="/wiki/Neapolitan_campaigns_of_Louis_the_Great" title="Neapolitan campaigns of Louis the Great">Hungarian invasion</a> of the <a href="/wiki/Kingdom_of_Naples" title="Kingdom of Naples">Kingdom of Naples</a>, which was a papal fief; the contest between <a href="/wiki/Louis_I_of_Hungary" title="Louis I of Hungary">Louis I of Hungary</a> and <a href="/wiki/Joanna_I_of_Naples" title="Joanna I of Naples">Joanna I of Naples</a>, accused of ordering the assassination of <a href="/wiki/Andrew,_Duke_of_Calabria" title="Andrew, Duke of Calabria">her husband</a> and the former's brother, concluded in 1348 in a trial held in Avignon, at which she was acquitted.<sup id="cite_ref-61" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-61">&#91;61&#93;</a></sup> Among the other benefits, Clement took advantage of the situation to obtain by her the rights over the city of <a href="/wiki/Avignon" title="Avignon">Avignon</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-EB1911_60-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-EB1911-60">&#91;60&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Pope Clement was also involved in disputes with King <a href="/wiki/Edward_III_of_England" title="Edward III of England">Edward III of England</a> as a result of the latter's encroachments on ecclesiastical jurisdiction.  He also faced problems with the kings of <a href="/wiki/Crown_of_Castile" title="Crown of Castile">Castile</a> and <a href="/wiki/Aragon" title="Aragon">Aragon</a>.  His  negotiations for reunion with the Armenians and the Byzantine emperor, <a href="/wiki/John_VI_Kantakouzenos" title="John VI Kantakouzenos">John VI Kantakouzenos</a>, turned out to be fruitless.<sup id="cite_ref-EB1911_60-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-EB1911-60">&#91;60&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In Italy the Papacy faced a serious challenge to its authority with the commencement of <a href="/wiki/Cola_di_Rienzo" title="Cola di Rienzo">Cola di Rienzo</a>'s agitation in <a href="/wiki/Rome" title="Rome">Rome</a>. Pope Clement had appointed Cola to a civil position (Senator) at Rome, and, although at first approving of Rienzo's establishment of the tribunate, he later realized the implications of a permanent antagonist to papal government in the form of a popularly elected Tribune, and sent a Papal Legate who excommunicated Rienzo and, with the help of the aristocratic faction, drove him from the city in December 1347.<sup id="cite_ref-EB1911_60-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-EB1911-60">&#91;60&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Poland_and_Bohemia">Poland and Bohemia</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Clement_VI&amp;action=edit&amp;section=10" title="Edit section: Poland and Bohemia">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Clement also had warned King <a href="/wiki/Casimir_III_of_Poland" class="mw-redirect" title="Casimir III of Poland">Casimir III of Poland</a>, who was already under an interdict laid against him by the bishop of Kraków and the Apostolic See, because he had oppressed the Church of Kraków with intolerable burdens and then harassed the clergy who observed the interdict, that he was attracting more severe penalties to himself.<sup id="cite_ref-62" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-62">&#91;62&#93;</a></sup>  In 1345 Clement sent a nuncio to King Casimir and King John of Bohemia, soliciting them to make peace between themselves, and threatening that, if they rejected his pleas, he would anathematize them and bar them from the sacraments.<sup id="cite_ref-63" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-63">&#91;63&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Responding to numerous complaints against the highhanded behavior of the <a href="/wiki/Archbishop_of_Mainz" class="mw-redirect" title="Archbishop of Mainz">archbishop of Mainz</a>, Prague's <a href="/wiki/Metropolitan_bishop" title="Metropolitan bishop">metropolitan</a>, Clement made Prague an archbishopric on 30 April 1344, and assigned the <a href="/wiki/Bishopric_of_Olomouc" class="mw-redirect" title="Bishopric of Olomouc">Bishopric of Olomouc</a> as its suffragan.  The <a href="/wiki/Archbishop_of_Prague" class="mw-redirect" title="Archbishop of Prague">archbishop of Prague</a> acquired the right to crown the <a href="/wiki/King_of_Bohemia" class="mw-redirect" title="King of Bohemia">king of Bohemia</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-64" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-64">&#91;64&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Private_life">Private life</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Clement_VI&amp;action=edit&amp;section=11" title="Edit section: Private life">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Unlike the Cistercian Benedict XII,  the Benedictine Clement VI was devoted to an openhanded and generous lifestyle, and the treasury which he inherited from his predecessor made that lifestyle possible.  He claimed to have "lived as a sinner among sinners" in his own words.<sup id="cite_ref-65" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-65">&#91;65&#93;</a></sup> During his pontificate, he added a new chapel to the Papal Palace and dedicated it to <a href="/wiki/St._Peter" class="mw-redirect" title="St. Peter">St. Peter</a>. He <a href="/wiki/Commission_(art)" title="Commission (art)">commissioned</a> the artist <a href="/wiki/Matteo_Giovanetti" title="Matteo Giovanetti">Matteo Giovanetti</a> of <a href="/wiki/Viterbo" title="Viterbo">Viterbo</a> to paint common hunting and fishing scenes on the walls of the existing papal chapels, and purchased enormous tapestries to decorate the stone walls. To bring good music to the celebrations, he recruited musicians from northern France, especially from <a href="/wiki/Li%C3%A8ge" title="Liège">Liège</a>, who cultivated the <a href="/wiki/Ars_Nova" class="mw-redirect" title="Ars Nova">Ars Nova</a> style. He liked music so much that he kept composers and theorists close to him throughout his entire pontificate, <a href="/wiki/Philippe_de_Vitry" title="Philippe de Vitry">Philippe de Vitry</a> being among the more famous. The first two payments he made after his coronation were to musicians.<sup id="cite_ref-66" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-66">&#91;66&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span id="Death.2C_burial.2C_and_monument"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Death,_burial,_and_monument">Death, burial, and monument</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Clement_VI&amp;action=edit&amp;section=12" title="Edit section: Death, burial, and monument">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Abbaye_Saint_Robert_de_La_Chaise_Dieu-Gisant_de_Cl%C3%A9ment_VI_D-201121007.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/74/Abbaye_Saint_Robert_de_La_Chaise_Dieu-Gisant_de_Cl%C3%A9ment_VI_D-201121007.jpg/220px-Abbaye_Saint_Robert_de_La_Chaise_Dieu-Gisant_de_Cl%C3%A9ment_VI_D-201121007.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="147" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/74/Abbaye_Saint_Robert_de_La_Chaise_Dieu-Gisant_de_Cl%C3%A9ment_VI_D-201121007.jpg/330px-Abbaye_Saint_Robert_de_La_Chaise_Dieu-Gisant_de_Cl%C3%A9ment_VI_D-201121007.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/74/Abbaye_Saint_Robert_de_La_Chaise_Dieu-Gisant_de_Cl%C3%A9ment_VI_D-201121007.jpg/440px-Abbaye_Saint_Robert_de_La_Chaise_Dieu-Gisant_de_Cl%C3%A9ment_VI_D-201121007.jpg 2x" data-file-width="4200" data-file-height="2800" /></a><figcaption>Tomb of Clement VI</figcaption></figure>
<p>Clement had been ill for some time in 1352, not just with kidney stones, which had troubled him for many years, but also with a tumor, which broke out into an abscess with fever during his last week.<sup id="cite_ref-67" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-67">&#91;67&#93;</a></sup>  Pope Clement VI died on 6 December 1352, in the eleventh year of his reign. After his death, his Almoner, Pierre de Froideville, distributed the sum of 400 livres to the poor of Avignon,  and on the day of the solemn funeral another 40 livres were distributed during the procession to the Cathedral to the poor who were present. Clement left the reputation of "a fine gentleman, a prince munificent to profusion, a patron of the arts and learning, but no saint".<sup id="cite_ref-68" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-68">&#91;68&#93;</a></sup> His body was placed on exhibit in the <a href="/wiki/Avignon_Cathedral" title="Avignon Cathedral">Notre Dame-des-Doms</a>, where it was buried temporarily. Three months later, the body was transferred in a splendid procession to the abbey of <a href="/wiki/La_Chaise-Dieu" title="La Chaise-Dieu">La Chaise-Dieu</a>, passing through Le Puy on 6 April.<sup id="cite_ref-69" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-69">&#91;69&#93;</a></sup>  On arrival, the coffin was placed in the church of the Carmelites. Later in April it was permanently interred in a tomb in the center of the Choir of the Church.<sup id="cite_ref-Morganstern61_70-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Morganstern61-70">&#91;70&#93;</a></sup> The funeral procession was accompanied by his brother Count William Roger of Beaufort, and by the five cardinals who were his family members: Hugues Roger, Guillaume de la Jugié, Nicolas de Besse, Pierre Roger de Beaufort, and Guillaume d' Aigrefeuille.<sup id="cite_ref-71" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-71">&#91;71&#93;</a></sup>  In 1562, the tomb was attacked by the Huguenots and severely damaged, losing the forty-four statues of Clement's relatives which surrounded the sarcophagus. Only the sarcophagus and tomb cover survived, making the present tomb a mere shadow of its former architectural and decorative glory.<sup id="cite_ref-72" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-72">&#91;72&#93;</a></sup> The tomb cover, in white marble,<sup id="cite_ref-73" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-73">&#91;73&#93;</a></sup> was made by master sculptor Pierre Boye, and his two assistants Jean de Sanholis and Jean David. The construction of the tomb began in 1346, and was completed in 1351. It cost 3,500 <a href="/wiki/Florin" title="Florin">florins</a>, to which were added 120 <a href="/wiki/%C3%89cu" title="Écu">écus</a> d'or, as a gratuity for the master sculptor.<sup id="cite_ref-74" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-74">&#91;74&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="See_also">See also</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Clement_VI&amp;action=edit&amp;section=13" title="Edit section: See also">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>

<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="References">References</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Clement_VI&amp;action=edit&amp;section=14" title="Edit section: References">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Citation">Citation</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Clement_VI&amp;action=edit&amp;section=15" title="Edit section: Citation">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1011085734">.mw-parser-output .reflist{font-size:90%;margin-bottom:0.5em;list-style-type:decimal}.mw-parser-output .reflist .references{font-size:100%;margin-bottom:0;list-style-type:inherit}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns-2{column-width:30em}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns-3{column-width:25em}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns{margin-top:0.3em}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns ol{margin-top:0}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns li{page-break-inside:avoid;break-inside:avoid-column}.mw-parser-output .reflist-upper-alpha{list-style-type:upper-alpha}.mw-parser-output .reflist-upper-roman{list-style-type:upper-roman}.mw-parser-output .reflist-lower-alpha{list-style-type:lower-alpha}.mw-parser-output .reflist-lower-greek{list-style-type:lower-greek}.mw-parser-output .reflist-lower-roman{list-style-type:lower-roman}</style><div class="reflist">

Paris: Chez l'Auteur. pp.&#160;301–302, 315, 317.</cite><span title="ctx_ver=Z39.88-2004&amp;rft_val_fmt=info%3Aofi%2Ffmt%3Akev%3Amtx%3Abook&amp;rft.genre=book&amp;rft.btitle=Histoire+des+chancelliers+de+France+et+des+gardes+de+sceaux+de+France&amp;rft.place=Paris&amp;rft.pages=301-302%2C+315%2C+317&amp;rft.pub=Chez+l%27Auteur&amp;rft.date=1680&amp;rft.au=Fran%C3%A7ois+Du+Chesne&amp;rft_id=https%3A%2F%2Fbooks.google.com%2Fbooks%3Fid%3Dzu03BsHVde4C%26pg%3DPA877&amp;rfr_id=info%3Asid%2Fen.wikipedia.org%3APope+Clement+VI" class="Z3988"></span>  Duchesne allows that Pierre Roger might have been Garde de Sceaux, but he relies on the authority of others and has no documentary proof himself for that office or for the Chancellorship. <li id="cite_note-39"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-39">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><i>Praedecessores nostri nesciverunt esse Papa.</i> This statement has sometimes been generalized to apply to all papal actions, quite wrongly, and sometimes maliciously.  It applies to benefices granted by a pope to needy clerics.  See, e.g., <link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1133582631"><cite id="CITEREFAnn_Deeley1928" class="citation journal cs1">Ann Deeley (1928). "Papal Provision and Royal Rights of Patronage in the Early Fourteenth Century". <i>The English Historical Review</i>. <b>43</b> (172): 497–527. <a href="/wiki/JSTOR_(identifier)" class="mw-redirect" title="JSTOR (identifier)">JSTOR</a>&#160;<a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://www.jstor.org/stable/551827">551827</a>.</cite><span title="ctx_ver=Z39.88-2004&amp;rft_val_fmt=info%3Aofi%2Ffmt%3Akev%3Amtx%3Ajournal&amp;rft.genre=article&amp;rft.jtitle=The+English+Historical+Review&amp;rft.atitle=Papal+Provision+and+Royal+Rights+of+Patronage+in+the+Early+Fourteenth+Century&amp;rft.volume=43&amp;rft.issue=172&amp;rft.pages=497-527&amp;rft.date=1928&amp;rft_id=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.jstor.org%2Fstable%2F551827%23id-name%3DJSTOR&amp;rft.au=Ann+Deeley&amp;rfr_id=info%3Asid%2Fen.wikipedia.org%3APope+Clement+VI" class="Z3988"></span></span>
